Allied officials in Vienna, mean- 
while, took steps to assure Aus- 
trian civilians that their scant ra- 
tion of 1,550 calories would not be 
reduced, at least during February. 

It was decided that 6,000 tons of 
food imported into Austria by the 
United States for use in emergen- 
cies and about 4,000 tons of Aus- 
trian reserves, mostly in the Rus- 
sian zone, would be allocated to 
make up a 10,000-ton shortage in 
supplies needed to maintain the 
ration. 

A similar shortage is expected to 
occur monthly until the next har- 
Bvest because of the withdrawal of

Letter From Wedemeyer 

The man who wrote that. state- 
ment last year, Mrs. Lohmann ex- 
plained, had been a foreign pupil 
her husband’s at ‘the German 
War College when the world was 
still at peace. 
» The firm signature at the bottom 
was unmistakable: ‘Albert C. 


lieutenant general, 
United States Army.” 


Lohmann, a square-jawed, bald 
specialist in aerial reconnaissance, 
commanded the 14th Field Division 
of the German air force in Norway 
until he ‘was seriously wounded by 
an ammunition explosion in 1944. 
Released from the Garmisch war 
prisoner camp last December 21, 
he was the first high officer to ‘be 
freed outright by American mili- 
tary authorities since the decision 
of the International Military 
Tribunal at Nuernberg acquitting 
the German general staff of 
criminality as an organization. .

Only Four New Faces . 

Among the fifteen men in the 
Ministry, there were only four new 
faces. Five Cabinet posts had been 
abolished, reduced to subsecre- 
taries or consolidated. 

The. Christian Democrats, who 
held eleven of the 21 posts in the 
putgoing Cabinet, lost their abso- 
lute numerical superiority, but still

purse strings, controlled its for- 
eign commerce and its police, agri- 
culture and public education. -

eign Ministry, thus avoiding a 
Socialist: signature to the Italian 
peace treaty, scheduled to be 
signed next week in Paris.

Delays Trip To U.S. 


Berlin, Feb. 2 (?)—Konrad Card- 


tives in Germany survived the war. 
With Germany collapsing, Mrs. 
Lohmann and her daughter fled to 
the Austrian Tyrol, “but there, too, 
we were persecuted because we 
were considered simply Germ 
and were refused food rations.” 
Got Food From Wedemeyer. 
» Eventually given shelter in Stutt- 
gart, the women were joined by 
her son, who had been an invalid 
prisoner of. war, Mrs,. Lohmar7 
gave English lessons to support her 
family until her health broke last 


year. 
. “I wrote 22 letters to General 
Wedemeyer before one final 
reached him in China in Decembe 
1945, and after that he wrote both 
to me and my husband, letters 
that were kind and good,” she said. 
“Just before Christmas, a pack~ 
e of American food arrived fro 
him, It was very fortunate, because 
on Christmas Eve the doorbell rang 
downstairs and a voice called to 
me, and there stood my husband. 
“We shared General Wede- 
meyer’s food for a Christmas to- 
gether such as we had not dreamed 
to have again. ae 
“My husband is 51 and he has 
always been a soldier and it will be 
hard-for him to start a little busi- 
ness or something like that as a 
civilian, for we are penniless,” she 
said. “Yet we thank God .

nd peace, if the G people 
hoose.”

« OTTAWA, \(AP) ‘Prime 
Minister W enzie King 


suggested strongly tonight that if 
Canada is denied a full voice in the 
making of the peace with Germany, 


she may be reluctant to lend her} 


aid in future wars. 


Protesting anew the 


Canada in current negotiations in 
London toward making of the Ger- 
man peace treaty, King told parl- 
jament that “we were fighting to 
prevent two countries from domin+ 
ating the world, and we do not 
wish to see anyone, two, three or 
four countries dominate the peace.” 


He then made what was inter-' 
preted in some quarters as a threat 
to withhold aid in any future war. 


"T hope,” he said “that any pow- 
ers that have*to consider to what 
}extent other countries that were 
in the wat are going to have an ef- 
fective voice in the making of the 
peace will consider carefully the 
justice of the situation to begin 
with, and further, what possible 
consequences there will be in the 
future if all that has been con- 
tributed by other nations is not re- 
cognized as fully as it should be. 


“T fear greatly for the drying up 
of the springs of chivalry and of 
generosity if the great powers in 
the world today are not prepared 
to recognize what has been done 
from these sources by the other 
‘nations of thé world in helping to 
save the freedom of the -world.” 

King’s statement was the strong- 
est yet made in Canada’s protest 
over being denied a full voice in 
discussions: now under way in Lon- 
don among deputies of the four- 


‘| power foreign ministers.

WASHINGTON, Feb. 3 — (4B) 
President Truman asked Congress 
today to continue his wartime pow- 
ers to allocate any materials—that 
is, determine how they shall be 
shared in an emergency. 

Senator Taft (R-Ohio) immed- 
iately said “no.” He contended this 
grant of authority would be too 
sweeping, 

The President also asked contin- 
uance of his authority to ration su- 
gar and to impos@ certain import 
and export controls. Taft, chairman 
of the Senate Republican Steéring 
committee, agreed to this, © 

Dug to Expire March 31 

Mr. Truman’s requests were Coti- 
tained in°a message recommending 
the retention for another year of 
certain authorities contained in the 
“Secon@ War Powers Act” of 1942 
and the abandonment of others.’ 
The act is scheduled tog expire 
March 31. 


Most of the controls to be aban- 


Although I do not amticipate that; 
such an emergeticy will occur, it is 
imperative that the government 
should have ‘the power, during the 
remainder of the reconversion per- 
iod, to deal with major unforeseen 
contingencies of this character.” 
The president did not say just 
what he had in mind, but apparent- 
ly it was the possibility of a strike 


al emergenty that we do mot now}

this kind, but only if there is a dec-} 
j€ign affairs subcommittee trip he 


emergenéy. Orders were issued 
during the soft coal strike last De- 
cember for the allocation of coal, 
gas and electricity. 

“I am in favor of continuing the 
controls on sugar and maybe con- 
trols over some other commodities 


if they can be shown to be neces- 
sary,” Taft told a reporter. “But I 
won't go along with any grant of 
powers to meet ‘a national emerg- 
ency that we do not now foresee.’” 

Taft said the Republican major- 
ity plans to handle the continuance 
of controls on sugar and other 
items in individual bills dealing 
with each one, rather than in a 
general measure. .. 

Mr. Truman had nothing to say 
on that procedure, but he did urge 
that whatever-congress does, it do 
before the March 31 deadline. Per- 
haps with last summer’s OPA fight 
in mind, he wrote: 

“It is unsettling, both for busi- 
ness and for the general public, to 
be obliged to wait until the last 
possible moment for decision by the 
congress on emergency legislation.” 

March 31, incidentally, was the 
deadline set by John L. Lewis when 
he ordered the striking coal miners 
back to work in December. 

The Senate and House leaders of 
both parties conferred with Mr, 
Truman at the White House before 
the message was delivered at noon. 
It was discussed there, along with 
other matters. Senator Vandenberg.

THE NEW CABINET CONSISTS OF SEVEN CHRISTIAN DEMOCRATS; THREE commun-\ 
ISTS, THREE SOCIALISTS AND TWO INDEPENDENTSe IN THE OUTGOING GOVERNMENT 
THE CHRISTIAN DEMOCRATS HELD 11 OF THE 21 POSTS, BUT THEY STILL HOLD 
THE PREMIERSHIP AND SEVERAL HIGHLY IMPORTANT MINISTRIES~ 

POLITICAL DISAGREEMENT OVER THE SELECTIONS CONTINUED UNTIL EARLY

VE.

TO SUB SECRETARIATS, OR CONSOLIDATED. THERE WERE ONLY FOUR NEW FACES 
AMONG THE 15 MEN IN THE MINISTRY.
